# Pandas: Data Manipulation and Analysis

Pandas is an indispensable library in the Python ecosystem for data manipulation and analysis, forming the bedrock for many machine learning and data science workflows. Built on top of NumPy, Pandas introduces two fundamental data structures: \`Series\` and \`DataFrame\`, which are designed for handling structured data efficiently and intuitively. This section will provide a comprehensive dive into mastering these structures and the powerful operations they enable.

:::info The Power of Pandas
Pandas excels at handling tabular data, akin to spreadsheets or SQL tables, but with far greater flexibility and computational power. It streamlines common data tasks such as cleaning, transforming, aggregating, and merging, making it a cornerstone for exploratory data analysis (EDA) and data preparation.
:::

## 1. Mastering Pandas DataFrames and Series

At the heart of Pandas are its two primary data structures: \`Series\` and \`DataFrame\`. Understanding their creation, characteristics, and basic operations is paramount.

### 1.1. Series: The 1-Dimensional Labeled Array

A \`Series\` is a one-dimensional labeled array capable of holding any data type (integers, strings, floats, Python objects, etc.). It can be thought of as a single column of a spreadsheet or a NumPy array with an associated label for each element, called an \`index\`.

#### 1.1.1. Series Creation

\`Series\` can be created from various data sources:

*   **From a Python List:**

    \`\`\`python
    import pandas as pd

    data = [10, 20, 30, 40, 50]
    s = pd.Series(data)
    print(s)
    \`\`\`

    Output:
    \`\`\`
    0    10
    1    20
    2    30
    3    40
    4    50
    dtype: int64
    \`\`\`

*   **With a Custom Index:**

    \`\`\`python
    s_indexed = pd.Series(data, index=['a', 'b', 'c', 'd', 'e'])
    print(s_indexed)
    \`\`\`

    Output:
    \`\`\`
    a    10
    b    20
    c    30
    d    40
    e    50
    dtype: int64
    \`\`\`

*   **From a Dictionary:** The keys become the index, and the values become the data.

    \`\`\`python
    data_dict = {'Math': 90, 'Science': 85, 'English': 78, 'History': 92}
    s_dict = pd.Series(data_dict)
    print(s_dict)
    \`\`\`

    Output:
    \`\`\`
    Math       90
    Science    85
    English    78
    History    92
    dtype: int64
    \`\`\`

*   **From a Scalar Value:** Creates a Series with the scalar value repeated for the length of the index.

    \`\`\`python
    s_scalar = pd.Series(5, index=['x', 'y', 'z'])
    print(s_scalar)
    \`\`\`

    Output:
    \`\`\`
    x    5
    y    5
    z    5
    dtype: int64
    \`\`\`

#### 1.1.2. Series Attributes

Key attributes of a \`Series\` include:

*   \`.values\`: Returns the data as a NumPy array.
*   \`.index\`: Returns the index labels.
*   \`.dtype\`: Returns the data type of the elements.
*   \`.name\`: An optional name for the Series.

    \`\`\`python
    print(s_dict.values)
    print(s_dict.index)
    print(s_dict.dtype)
    s_dict.name = 'Student Scores'
    print(s_dict.name)
    \`\`\`

### 1.2. DataFrame: The 2-Dimensional Labeled Table

A \`DataFrame\` is a two-dimensional labeled data structure with columns of potentially different types. You can think of it as a dictionary of \`Series\` objects, where all \`Series\` share the same index. It is the most commonly used Pandas object.

#### 1.2.1. DataFrame Creation

\`DataFrame\`s can be created in several ways:

*   **From a Dictionary of Lists/NumPy Arrays:** Each key becomes a column name, and its corresponding list/array becomes the column's data.

    \`\`\`python
    data = {
        'Name': ['Alice', 'Bob', 'Charlie', 'David'],
        'Age': [24, 27, 22, 32],
        'City': ['New York', 'Los Angeles', 'Chicago', 'Houston']
    }
    df = pd.DataFrame(data)
    print(df)
    \`\`\`

    Output:
    \`\`\`
          Name  Age         City
    0    Alice   24     New York
    1      Bob   27  Los Angeles
    2  Charlie   22      Chicago
    3    David   32      Houston
    \`\`\`

*   **From a List of Dictionaries:** Each dictionary represents a row, with keys as column names.

    \`\`\`python
    data_list_dict = [
        {'Name': 'Eve', 'Age': 30, 'City': 'Miami'},
        {'Name': 'Frank', 'Age': 28, 'City': 'Seattle'}
    ]
    df_list_dict = pd.DataFrame(data_list_dict)
    print(df_list_dict)
    \`\`\`

    Output:
    \`\`\`
        Name  Age     City
    0    Eve   30    Miami
    1  Frank   28  Seattle
    \`\`\`

*   **From a CSV File (Common in ML/DS):**

    \`\`\`python
    # Assuming 'data.csv' exists with comma-separated values
    # df_csv = pd.read_csv('data.csv')
    # print(df_csv.head())
    \`\`\`

*   **From a NumPy Array:** Requires specifying column names and index.

    \`\`\`python
    import numpy as np
    np_data = np.array([[1, 2, 3], [4, 5, 6]])
    df_np = pd.DataFrame(np_data, columns=['ColA', 'ColB', 'ColC'], index=['Row1', 'Row2'])
    print(df_np)
    \`\`\`

    Output:
    \`\`\`
          ColA  ColB  ColC
    Row1     1     2     3
    Row2     4     5     6
    \`\`\`

#### 1.2.2. DataFrame Attributes

Important \`DataFrame\` attributes include:

*   \`.shape\`: Returns a tuple representing the dimensionality (rows, columns).
*   \`.columns\`: Returns column labels.
*   \`.index\`: Returns row labels.
*   \`.dtypes\`: Returns the data type of each column.
*   \`.info()\`: Provides a concise summary of the DataFrame, including dtypes, non-null values, and memory usage.
*   \`.describe()\`: Generates descriptive statistics of numerical columns (count, mean, std, min, max, quartiles).

    \`\`\`python
    print(df.shape)
    print(df.columns)
    print(df.index)
    print(df.dtypes)
    df.info()
    print(df.describe())
    \`\`\`

## 2. Indexing (\`loc\`, \`iloc\`), Selection, Filtering, and Sorting

Accessing and manipulating specific subsets of data is fundamental. Pandas offers powerful and flexible methods for this.

### 2.1. Selection

#### 2.1.1. Selecting Columns

You can select one or more columns from a \`DataFrame\`:

*   **Single Column (returns a Series):**

    \`\`\`python
    ages = df['Age']
    print(type(ages))
    print(ages)
    \`\`\`

*   **Multiple Columns (returns a DataFrame):**

    \`\`\`python
    name_city = df[['Name', 'City']]
    print(type(name_city))
    print(name_city)
    \`\`\`

#### 2.1.2. Selecting Rows

*   **Using Slicing (by integer position):** Similar to Python lists.

    \`\`\`python
    first_two_rows = df[0:2]
    print(first_two_rows)
    \`\`\`

### 2.2. Indexing with \`loc\` and \`iloc\`

These are the primary methods for label-based (\`loc\`) and integer-position-based (\`iloc\`) indexing.

#### 2.2.1. \`loc\` (Label-based Indexing)

\`loc\` uses explicit index labels for rows and column labels for columns.

*   **Selecting a single row by label:**

    \`\`\`python
    row_0_loc = df.loc[0] # Even if index is integer, loc treats it as label
    print(row_0_loc)
    \`\`\`

*   **Selecting multiple rows by label:**

    \`\`\`python
    rows_0_1_loc = df.loc[0:1] # Inclusive of end label
    print(rows_0_1_loc)
    \`\`\`

*   **Selecting specific cells by row and column labels:** \`df.loc[row_label, column_label]\`

    \`\`\`python
    alice_age = df.loc[0, 'Age']
    print(f"Alice's age: {alice_age}")

    sub_df = df.loc[[0, 2], ['Name', 'City']]
    print(sub_df)
    \`\`\`

*   **Selecting all rows for specific columns:** \`df.loc[:, column_labels]\`

    \`\`\`python
    all_names_cities = df.loc[:, ['Name', 'City']]
    print(all_names_cities)
    \`\`\`

#### 2.2.2. \`iloc\` (Integer-location based Indexing)

\`iloc\` uses integer positions for both rows and columns, similar to NumPy array indexing.

*   **Selecting a single row by position:**

    \`\`\`python
    row_0_iloc = df.iloc[0]
    print(row_0_iloc)
    \`\`\`

*   **Selecting multiple rows by position (exclusive of end position):**

    \`\`\`python
    rows_0_1_iloc = df.iloc[0:2]
    print(rows_0_1_iloc)
    \`\`\`

*   **Selecting specific cells by row and column positions:** \`df.iloc[row_pos, col_pos]\`

    \`\`\`python
    bob_city = df.iloc[1, 2] # Row 1, Column 2 (City)
    print(f"Bob's city: {bob_city}")

    sub_df_iloc = df.iloc[[0, 2], [0, 2]] # Rows 0 and 2, Columns 0 (Name) and 2 (City)
    print(sub_df_iloc)
    \`\`\`

*   **Selecting all rows for specific column positions:** \`df.iloc[:, col_positions]\`

    \`\`\`python
    all_names_cities_iloc = df.iloc[:, [0, 2]]
    print(all_names_cities_iloc)
    \`\`\`

:::tip \`loc\` vs. \`iloc\`
*   Use \`loc\` when you know the *labels* of your rows/columns.
*   Use \`iloc\` when you know the *integer positions* of your rows/columns.
*   Be cautious with \`loc\` when your index is integer-based; \`df.loc[0]\` refers to the label \`0\`, not necessarily the first position.
:::

### 2.3. Filtering (Boolean Indexing)

Filtering allows you to select rows based on one or more conditions. This is achieved by passing a boolean \`Series\` (or array) to the \`DataFrame\`'s \`[]\` operator or to \`loc\`.

*   **Filtering by a single condition:**

    \`\`\`python
    # Select people older than 25
    older_than_25 = df[df['Age'] > 25]
    print(older_than_25)
    \`\`\`

*   **Filtering by multiple conditions (using \`&\` for AND, \`|\` for OR, \`~\` for NOT):**
    *   **Important:** Each condition must be enclosed in parentheses.

    \`\`\`python
    # People older than 25 AND living in New York or Chicago
    filtered_df = df[(df['Age'] > 25) & ((df['City'] == 'New York') | (df['City'] == 'Chicago'))]
    print(filtered_df)
    \`\`\`

*   **Using \`isin()\` for multiple categorical values:**

    \`\`\`python
    # People living in New York or Los Angeles
    city_filter = df[df['City'].isin(['New York', 'Los Angeles'])]
    print(city_filter)
    \`\`\`

### 2.4. Sorting

Sorting data is crucial for understanding distributions and preparing for analysis.

*   **Sorting by a single column:** \`df.sort_values(by='column_name', ascending=True/False)\`

    \`\`\`python
    # Sort by Age in ascending order
    df_sorted_age = df.sort_values(by='Age')
    print(df_sorted_age)

    # Sort by Age in descending order
    df_sorted_age_desc = df.sort_values(by='Age', ascending=False)
    print(df_sorted_age_desc)
    \`\`\`

*   **Sorting by multiple columns:** The order of columns in the list determines the sorting hierarchy.

    \`\`\`python
    # Sort by City (ascending), then by Age (descending)
    df_sorted_multi = df.sort_values(by=['City', 'Age'], ascending=[True, False])
    print(df_sorted_multi)
    \`\`\`

*   **Sorting by index:** \`df.sort_index()\`

    \`\`\`python
    # Create a DataFrame with a non-sorted index
    df_unsorted_index = pd.DataFrame({'Value': [10, 20, 30]}, index=[2, 0, 1])
    print("Unsorted Index:")
    print(df_unsorted_index)

    df_sorted_index = df_unsorted_index.sort_index()
    print("\nSorted Index:")
    print(df_sorted_index)
    \`\`\`

## 3. Advanced Data Cleaning Techniques

Real-world data is rarely pristine. Data cleaning is often the most time-consuming part of a data science project. Pandas provides robust tools for handling common data quality issues.

### 3.1. Handling Missing Data

Missing data (often represented as \`NaN\` - Not a Number) can significantly impact analysis and model performance.

#### 3.1.1. Identifying Missing Data

*   \`.isnull()\` / \`.isna()\`: Returns a boolean DataFrame indicating where values are missing.
*   \`.notnull()\` / \`.notna()\`: Returns a boolean DataFrame indicating where values are *not* missing.
*   \`.isnull().sum()\`: Counts missing values per column.

    \`\`\`python
    data_missing = {
        'A': [1, 2, np.nan, 4],
        'B': [5, np.nan, 7, 8],
        'C': [9, 10, 11, np.nan]
    }
    df_missing = pd.DataFrame(data_missing)
    print("Original DataFrame with missing values:")
    print(df_missing)

    print("\nMissing values (boolean DataFrame):")
    print(df_missing.isnull())

    print("\nNumber of missing values per column:")
    print(df_missing.isnull().sum())

    print("\nTotal missing values in DataFrame:")
    print(df_missing.isnull().sum().sum())
    \`\`\`

#### 3.1.2. Dropping Missing Data (\`dropna()\`)

*   \`df.dropna(axis=0)\`: Drops rows containing *any* \`NaN\` value. (\`axis=0\` is default)
*   \`df.dropna(axis=1)\`: Drops columns containing *any* \`NaN\` value.
*   \`df.dropna(how='all')\`: Drops rows/columns only if *all* values are \`NaN\`.
*   \`df.dropna(thresh=n)\`: Drops rows/columns that have less than \`n\` non-NaN values.

    \`\`\`python
    print("\nDataFrame after dropping rows with any NaN:")
    print(df_missing.dropna())

    print("\nDataFrame after dropping columns with any NaN:")
    print(df_missing.dropna(axis=1))

    # Example for how='all'
    df_all_nan = pd.DataFrame({'A': [1, np.nan], 'B': [np.nan, np.nan]})
    print("\nDataFrame with some all-NaN rows/cols:")
    print(df_all_nan)
    print("\nDropping rows where all values are NaN:")
    print(df_all_nan.dropna(how='all'))
    \`\`\`

#### 3.1.3. Filling Missing Data (\`fillna()\`)

*   **Filling with a scalar value:**

    \`\`\`python
    print("\nDataFrame after filling NaN with 0:")
    print(df_missing.fillna(0))
    \`\`\`

*   **Filling with a statistical measure (mean, median, mode):**

    \`\`\`python
    # Fill 'A' with its mean, 'B' with its median
    df_filled_stats = df_missing.copy()
    df_filled_stats['A'] = df_filled_stats['A'].fillna(df_filled_stats['A'].mean())
    df_filled_stats['B'] = df_filled_stats['B'].fillna(df_filled_stats['B'].median())
    print("\nDataFrame after filling with column-specific stats:")
    print(df_filled_stats)
    \`\`\`

*   **Forward fill (\`ffill\` / \`pad\`):** Propagates the last valid observation forward to next valid.

    \`\`\`python
    print("\nDataFrame after forward fill:")
    print(df_missing.fillna(method='ffill'))
    \`\`\`

*   **Backward fill (\`bfill\` / \`backfill\`):** Propagates the next valid observation backward to previous valid.

    \`\`\`python
    print("\nDataFrame after backward fill:")
    print(df_missing.fillna(method='bfill'))
    \`\`\`

### 3.2. Handling Duplicates

Duplicate rows can bias analysis. Pandas offers methods to identify and remove them.

#### 3.2.1. Identifying Duplicates (\`duplicated()\`)

\`df.duplicated()\` returns a boolean Series indicating whether each row is a duplicate of a previous row.

*   \`subset\`: List of column names to consider for identifying duplicates.
*   \`keep\`: \`'first'\` (default), \`'last'\`, or \`False\`.
    *   \`'first'\`: Marks duplicates as \`True\` except for the first occurrence.
    *   \`'last'\`: Marks duplicates as \`True\` except for the last occurrence.
    *   \`False\`: Marks *all* duplicates as \`True\`.

    \`\`\`python
    data_dup = {'A': [1, 2, 2, 3, 4, 4],
                'B': ['x', 'y', 'y', 'z', 'a', 'a'],
                'C': [10, 20, 20, 30, 40, 50]}
    df_dup = pd.DataFrame(data_dup)
    print("Original DataFrame with duplicates:")
    print(df_dup)

    print("\nDuplicated rows (keeping first occurrence as non-duplicate):")
    print(df_dup.duplicated())

    print("\nDuplicated rows (considering only columns A and B):")
    print(df_dup.duplicated(subset=['A', 'B']))
    \`\`\`

#### 3.2.2. Dropping Duplicates (\`drop_duplicates()\`)

\`df.drop_duplicates()\` removes duplicate rows based on the \`subset\` and \`keep\` parameters.

    \`\`\`python
    print("\nDataFrame after dropping duplicates (default: keep='first'):")
    print(df_dup.drop_duplicates())

    print("\nDataFrame after dropping duplicates (keep='last'):")
    print(df_dup.drop_duplicates(keep='last'))

    print("\nDataFrame after dropping duplicates (considering A and B, keep='first'):")
    print(df_dup.drop_duplicates(subset=['A', 'B']))
    \`\`\`

### 3.3. Type Conversion

Ensuring columns have the correct data type is crucial for memory efficiency and correct operations.

*   \`.astype()\`: Converts a Series or DataFrame column to a specified data type.
*   \`pd.to_numeric()\`, \`pd.to_datetime()\`, \`pd.to_timedelta()\`: Specialized functions for robust type conversion, especially with potential errors.

    \`\`\`python
    df_types = pd.DataFrame({
        'ID': ['101', '102', '103'],
        'Value': ['5', '10', '15'],
        'Date_Str': ['2023-01-01', '2023-01-02', '2023-01-03'],
        'Is_Active': [1, 0, 1]
    })
    print("Original dtypes:")
    print(df_types.dtypes)

    # Convert 'ID' to int, 'Value' to float
    df_types['ID'] = df_types['ID'].astype(int)
    df_types['Value'] = df_types['Value'].astype(float)
    df_types['Is_Active'] = df_types['Is_Active'].astype(bool)

    # Convert 'Date_Str' to datetime objects
    df_types['Date_Str'] = pd.to_datetime(df_types['Date_Str'])

    print("\nNew dtypes:")
    print(df_types.dtypes)

    # Handling errors in conversion: 'coerce' turns unparseable values into NaN
    df_bad_num = pd.DataFrame({'Col': ['1', '2', 'abc', '4']})
    df_bad_num['Col_Numeric'] = pd.to_numeric(df_bad_num['Col'], errors='coerce')
    print("\nDataFrame with coerced numeric conversion:")
    print(df_bad_num)
    \`\`\`

## 4. Data Transformation

Data transformation involves changing the structure or content of your data to make it more suitable for analysis.

### 4.1. \`apply()\` and \`map()\`

These functions are powerful for applying custom logic element-wise or column-wise.

#### 4.1.1. \`apply()\`

*   **On a Series:** Applies a function to each element.
*   **On a DataFrame:**
    *   \`axis=0\` (default): Applies a function to each column (Series).
    *   \`axis=1\`: Applies a function to each row (Series).

    \`\`\`python
    df_transform = pd.DataFrame({
        'A': [1, 2, 3],
        'B': [4, 5, 6],
        'C': ['apple', 'banana', 'cherry']
    })

    # Apply on a Series: Square each element in column 'A'
    df_transform['A_squared'] = df_transform['A'].apply(lambda x: x**2)
    print("\nAfter applying square function to column A:")
    print(df_transform)

    # Apply on a DataFrame row-wise: Sum of A and B for each row
    df_transform['Sum_AB'] = df_transform[['A', 'B']].apply(lambda row: row['A'] + row['B'], axis=1)
    print("\nAfter applying sum function row-wise:")
    print(df_transform)

    # Apply on a DataFrame column-wise: Get length of string column
    df_transform['C_length'] = df_transform['C'].apply(len)
    print("\nAfter applying len function to column C:")
    print(df_transform)
    \`\`\`

#### 4.1.2. \`map()\`

\`map()\` is available on a Series and is used for substituting each value in a Series with another value. It's particularly useful for mapping categorical values or creating new columns based on existing ones.

*   **Using a dictionary for mapping:**

    \`\`\`python
    status_map = {'apple': 'Fruit', 'banana': 'Fruit', 'cherry': 'Fruit', 'grape': 'Fruit'}
    df_transform['C_Category'] = df_transform['C'].map(status_map)
    print("\nAfter mapping column C to categories:")
    print(df_transform)
    \`\`\`

*   **Using a function for mapping:** (Similar to \`apply\`, but \`map\` is Series-specific and often faster for element-wise operations)

    \`\`\`python
    df_transform['C_Upper'] = df_transform['C'].map(str.upper)
    print("\nAfter mapping column C to uppercase:")
    print(df_transform)
    \`\`\`

:::note \`apply\` vs. \`map\` vs. \`applymap\`
*   \`Series.map()\`: Element-wise operation on a Series using a dictionary, Series, or function.
*   \`DataFrame.apply()\`: Row-wise or column-wise operation on a DataFrame, returning a Series or DataFrame.
*   \`DataFrame.applymap()\`: Element-wise operation on a DataFrame (applies a function to every element), less common in modern Pandas in favor of \`apply\` with \`axis=0/1\` or vectorized operations.
:::

### 4.2. \`groupby()\`: Split-Apply-Combine

\`groupby()\` is one of the most powerful features in Pandas, allowing you to split data into groups based on some criteria, apply a function to each group independently, and then combine the results. This follows the "Split-Apply-Combine" strategy.

\`\`\`python
data_sales = {
    'Region': ['East', 'West', 'East', 'West', 'East', 'South', 'South'],
    'Product': ['A', 'B', 'A', 'C', 'B', 'A', 'C'],
    'Sales': [100, 150, 120, 200, 130, 90, 180],
    'Quantity': [10, 15, 12, 20, 13, 9, 18]
}
df_sales = pd.DataFrame(data_sales)
print("Original Sales DataFrame:")
print(df_sales)

# Group by 'Region' and calculate the sum of 'Sales'
regional_sales = df_sales.groupby('Region')['Sales'].sum()
print("\nTotal Sales by Region:")
print(regional_sales)

# Group by 'Region' and 'Product' and calculate mean of 'Quantity'
avg_quantity = df_sales.groupby(['Region', 'Product'])['Quantity'].mean()
print("\nAverage Quantity by Region and Product:")
print(avg_quantity)

# Applying multiple aggregation functions
grouped_stats = df_sales.groupby('Region').agg(
    Total_Sales=('Sales', 'sum'),
    Avg_Quantity=('Quantity', 'mean'),
    Num_Transactions=('Region', 'count')
)
print("\nMultiple Aggregations by Region:")
print(grouped_stats)

# Applying different functions to different columns
grouped_custom_agg = df_sales.groupby('Region').agg({
    'Sales': ['sum', 'mean'],
    'Quantity': 'max'
})
print("\nCustom Aggregations:")
print(grouped_custom_agg)
\`\`\`

### 4.3. Pivot Tables (\`pivot_table()\`)

Pivot tables are a way to summarize and rearrange data in a tabular format, often used for creating cross-tabulations. They take column values and turn them into new column headers.

\`\`\`python
# Reusing df_sales

# Pivot table: Sales by Region and Product
pivot_sales = df_sales.pivot_table(values='Sales', index='Region', columns='Product', aggfunc='sum')
print("\nPivot Table: Total Sales by Region and Product:")
print(pivot_sales)

# Fill missing values (NaN) with 0 for better readability
pivot_sales_filled = pivot_sales.fillna(0)
print("\nPivot Table (NaN filled with 0):")
print(pivot_sales_filled)

# Pivot with multiple aggregation functions
pivot_multi_agg = df_sales.pivot_table(
    values=['Sales', 'Quantity'],
    index='Region',
    columns='Product',
    aggfunc={'Sales': 'sum', 'Quantity': 'mean'}
)
print("\nPivot Table with multiple aggregations:")
print(pivot_multi_agg)
\`\`\`

### 4.4. Reshaping Data: \`melt()\`, \`stack()\`, \`unstack()\`

These functions are used to transform the layout of DataFrames between "wide" and "long" formats.

#### 4.4.1. \`melt()\`: From Wide to Long Format

\`melt()\` transforms a DataFrame from a "wide" format (where columns represent categories) to a "long" format (where categories become rows). This is often useful for visualization libraries like Seaborn.

\`\`\`python
df_wide = pd.DataFrame({
    'Student': ['Alice', 'Bob'],
    'Math': [90, 85],
    'Science': [78, 92],
    'English': [88, 75]
})
print("Original Wide DataFrame:")
print(df_wide)

df_long = df_wide.melt(id_vars=['Student'], var_name='Subject', value_name='Score')
print("\nMelted Long DataFrame:")
print(df_long)
\`\`\`

#### 4.4.2. \`stack()\` and \`unstack()\`: Pivoting with MultiIndex

\`stack()\` and \`unstack()\` are primarily used with MultiIndex (hierarchical index) DataFrames and Series.

*   \`stack()\`: Pivots a level of the column labels to become the innermost level of the row index, making the DataFrame "taller" (more rows, fewer columns).
*   \`unstack()\`: Pivots a level of the row index to become a new level of the column index, making the DataFrame "wider" (fewer rows, more columns).

\`\`\`python
# Create a MultiIndex DataFrame
idx = pd.MultiIndex.from_product([['A', 'B'], [1, 2]], names=['Level1', 'Level2'])
df_multi = pd.DataFrame(np.random.rand(4, 2), index=idx, columns=['ColX', 'ColY'])
print("Original MultiIndex DataFrame:")
print(df_multi)

# Stack: Move 'Level2' from row index to column index
df_stacked = df_multi.stack()
print("\nStacked DataFrame (Level2 moved to columns):")
print(df_stacked)
print(type(df_stacked)) # A Series with a MultiIndex

# Unstack: Move 'Level1' from row index to column index (from a Series)
df_unstacked_from_series = df_stacked.unstack(level='Level1')
print("\nUnstacked from Series (Level1 moved to columns):")
print(df_unstacked_from_series)

# Unstack directly from DataFrame (move 'Level2' of index to columns)
df_unstacked_from_df = df_multi.unstack(level='Level2')
print("\nUnstacked from DataFrame (Level2 of index moved to columns):")
print(df_unstacked_from_df)
\`\`\`

## 5. Merging, Joining, and Concatenating DataFrames

Combining DataFrames is a common operation when working with data from multiple sources. Pandas provides functions similar to SQL joins.

### 5.1. \`concat()\`: Stacking DataFrames

\`pd.concat()\` stacks DataFrames either vertically (rows) or horizontally (columns).

*   **Concatenating rows (\`axis=0\`, default):** Appends rows from one DataFrame to another.

    \`\`\`python
    df1 = pd.DataFrame({'A': ['A0', 'A1'], 'B': ['B0', 'B1']}, index=[0, 1])
    df2 = pd.DataFrame({'A': ['A2', 'A3'], 'B': ['B2', 'B3']}, index=[2, 3])
    print("df1:\n", df1)
    print("df2:\n", df2)

    result_rows = pd.concat([df1, df2])
    print("\nConcatenated Rows (axis=0):\n", result_rows)

    # Handling non-matching columns: Fills with NaN by default
    df3 = pd.DataFrame({'C': ['C4', 'C5'], 'D': ['D4', 'D5']}, index=[4, 5])
    result_rows_mismatch = pd.concat([df1, df3])
    print("\nConcatenated Rows with Mismatching Columns:\n", result_rows_mismatch)
    \`\`\`

*   **Concatenating columns (\`axis=1\`):** Appends columns from one DataFrame to another.

    \`\`\`python
    result_cols = pd.concat([df1, df2], axis=1)
    print("\nConcatenated Columns (axis=1):\n", result_cols)

    # Handling non-matching indices: Fills with NaN by default
    df4 = pd.DataFrame({'C': ['C0', 'C1'], 'D': ['D0', 'D1']}, index=[0, 1])
    df5 = pd.DataFrame({'E': ['E2', 'E3'], 'F': ['F2', 'F3']}, index=[2, 3])
    result_cols_mismatch = pd.concat([df4, df5], axis=1)
    print("\nConcatenated Columns with Mismatching Indices:\n", result_cols_mismatch)
    \`\`\`

:::tip Handling Indices in \`concat\`
*   \`ignore_index=True\`: Resets the index of the resulting DataFrame to a default integer index. Useful when original indices are not meaningful or are duplicates.
*   \`keys\`: Creates a hierarchical index to identify which original DataFrame each row came from.
:::

### 5.2. \`merge()\`: Combining DataFrames based on Common Columns (SQL-style Joins)

\`pd.merge()\` combines DataFrames based on common columns or indices, similar to database joins.

\`\`\`python
df_customers = pd.DataFrame({
    'customer_id': [1, 2, 3, 4],
    'name': ['Alice', 'Bob', 'Charlie', 'David']
})

df_orders = pd.DataFrame({
    'order_id': [101, 102, 103, 104, 105],
    'customer_id': [2, 1, 3, 2, 5], # Customer 5 is not in df_customers
    'product': ['Laptop', 'Mouse', 'Keyboard', 'Monitor', 'Webcam']
})

print("df_customers:\n", df_customers)
print("df_orders:\n", df_orders)
\`\`\`

#### 5.2.1. Types of Merges

*   **Inner Join (\`how='inner'\`, default):** Returns only the rows where the merge key(s) exist in *both* DataFrames.

    \`\`\`python
    inner_join = pd.merge(df_customers, df_orders, on='customer_id', how='inner')
    print("\nInner Join (matching customer_id in both):\n", inner_join)
    \`\`\`

*   **Left Join (\`how='left'\`):** Returns all rows from the left DataFrame and the matched rows from the right DataFrame. If no match, \`NaN\` is filled for the right DataFrame's columns.

    \`\`\`python
    left_join = pd.merge(df_customers, df_orders, on='customer_id', how='left')
    print("\nLeft Join (all customers, matching orders):\n", left_join)
    \`\`\`

*   **Right Join (\`how='right'\`):** Returns all rows from the right DataFrame and the matched rows from the left DataFrame. If no match, \`NaN\` is filled for the left DataFrame's columns.

    \`\`\`python
    right_join = pd.merge(df_customers, df_orders, on='customer_id', how='right')
    print("\nRight Join (all orders, matching customers):\n", right_join)
    \`\`\`

*   **Outer Join (\`how='outer'\`):** Returns all rows when there is a match in *either* the left or right DataFrame. Fills \`NaN\` where no match exists.

    \`\`\`python
    outer_join = pd.merge(df_customers, df_orders, on='customer_id', how='outer')
    print("\nOuter Join (all records from both, matched or not):\n", outer_join)
    \`\`\`

#### 5.2.2. Merging on Multiple Keys

\`\`\`python
df_sales_regions = pd.DataFrame({
    'Region': ['East', 'West', 'East'],
    'Product': ['A', 'B', 'A'],
    'Salesman': ['John', 'Jane', 'John']
})

df_salesman_info = pd.DataFrame({
    'Salesman': ['John', 'Jane', 'Peter'],
    'Territory': ['NY', 'CA', 'TX']
})

merged_sales = pd.merge(df_sales_regions, df_salesman_info, on='Salesman')
print("\nMerged on 'Salesman':\n", merged_sales)
\`\`\`

#### 5.2.3. Merging on Different Column Names

If the columns to merge on have different names in each DataFrame, use \`left_on\` and \`right_on\`.

\`\`\`python
df_employees = pd.DataFrame({
    'emp_id': [1, 2, 3],
    'name': ['E1', 'E2', 'E3']
})

df_salaries = pd.DataFrame({
    'employee_id': [1, 2, 4],
    'salary': [50000, 60000, 70000]
})

merged_diff_names = pd.merge(df_employees, df_salaries, left_on='emp_id', right_on='employee_id', how='outer')
print("\nMerged on different column names:\n", merged_diff_names)
\`\`\`

### 5.3. \`join()\`: Combining DataFrames based on Index

\`df.join()\` is a convenient method for combining DataFrames based on their *indices*. By default, it performs a left join.

\`\`\`python
df_left = pd.DataFrame({'A': ['A0', 'A1'], 'B': ['B0', 'B1']}, index=['K0', 'K1'])
df_right = pd.DataFrame({'C': ['C0', 'C2'], 'D': ['D0', 'D2']}, index=['K0', 'K2'])

print("df_left:\n", df_left)
print("df_right:\n", df_right)

joined_df = df_left.join(df_right) # Default is left join
print("\nJoined (default left join):\n", joined_df)

joined_outer = df_left.join(df_right, how='outer')
print("\nJoined (outer join):\n", joined_outer)

# Joining on a column
df_left_col_join = pd.DataFrame({'key': ['K0', 'K1', 'K2'], 'A': ['A0', 'A1', 'A2']})
df_right_col_join = pd.DataFrame({'key': ['K0', 'K1', 'K3'], 'B': ['B0', 'B1', 'B3']})

# To join on a column using .join(), one of the DataFrames must have that column as its index
# Or you can use 'on' parameter if the right DataFrame has a matching index
joined_on_col = df_left_col_join.set_index('key').join(df_right_col_join.set_index('key'), how='inner')
print("\nJoined on 'key' column (via set_index):\n", joined_on_col)
\`\`\`

:::warning \`merge\` vs. \`join\`
*   \`merge()\` is more general-purpose, allowing joins on columns or indices with full control over join types.
*   \`join()\` is a DataFrame method primarily for joining on indices, which can be convenient for simple index-based lookups. For joining on columns, it often requires \`set_index()\` first, or using the \`on\` parameter if the right DataFrame's index matches the left DataFrame's column. For complex column-based joins, \`merge()\` is usually preferred.
:::

## 6. Time Series Functionality

Pandas has excellent support for time series data, making it a powerful tool for financial, sensor, and other sequential data analysis.

### 6.1. Creating Date and Time Series

*   \`pd.to_datetime()\`: Converts various date/time formats to datetime objects.
*   \`pd.date_range()\`: Generates a fixed frequency DatetimeIndex.

    \`\`\`python
    dates = pd.to_datetime(['2023-01-01', '2023-01-02 10:30', 'Jan 3, 2023'])
    print("Converted dates:\n", dates)

    date_range_series = pd.Series(np.random.randn(10), index=pd.date_range('2023-01-01', periods=10, freq='D'))
    print("\nDate Range Series:\n", date_range_series)
    \`\`\`

### 6.2. Time-based Indexing and Selection

When a DataFrame or Series has a \`DatetimeIndex\`, you can use powerful time-based indexing.

\`\`\`python
ts = pd.Series(np.random.randn(100), index=pd.date_range('2023-01-01', periods=100, freq='H'))
print("Time Series head:\n", ts.head())

# Select data for a specific date
print("\nData for '2023-01-01':\n", ts['2023-01-01'])

# Select data for a month
print("\nData for '2023-01' (first 5):\n", ts['2023-01'].head())

# Select a date range
print("\nData from '2023-01-01 02:00' to '2023-01-01 05:00':\n", ts['2023-01-01 02:00':'2023-01-01 05:00'])
\`\`\`

### 6.3. Resampling

Resampling changes the frequency of time series data (e.g., from hourly to daily).

*   **Downsampling:** Aggregating data to a lower frequency (e.g., hourly to daily). Requires an aggregation function.
*   **Upsampling:** Increasing data frequency (e.g., daily to hourly). Requires a filling method.

\`\`\`python
# Downsampling: Hourly data to daily mean
daily_mean = ts.resample('D').mean()
print("\nDaily Mean (first 5):\n", daily_mean.head())

# Downsampling: Weekly sum
weekly_sum = ts.resample('W').sum()
print("\nWeekly Sum (first 5):\n", weekly_sum.head())

# Upsampling: Daily data to hourly, filling with forward fill
daily_data = pd.Series([10, 20, 30], index=pd.to_datetime(['2023-01-01', '2023-01-02', '2023-01-03']))
hourly_data_ffill = daily_data.resample('H').ffill()
print("\nUpsampled Hourly (ffill) from Daily data (first 5):\n", hourly_data_ffill.head())
\`\`\`

### 6.4. Time Series Components

Pandas provides easy access to date/time components.

\`\`\`python
dt_index = pd.date_range('2023-01-01', periods=5, freq='D')
df_ts_components = pd.DataFrame({'value': range(5)}, index=dt_index)

print("Year:", df_ts_components.index.year)
print("Month:", df_ts_components.index.month)
print("Day:", df_ts_components.index.day)
print("Day of Week:", df_ts_components.index.dayofweek) # Monday=0, Sunday=6
print("Week of Year:", df_ts_components.index.isocalendar().week)
print("Is Weekend:", df_ts_components.index.dayofweek.isin([5, 6]))
\`\`\`

## 7. Performance Optimization in Pandas

While Pandas is generally efficient, large datasets can benefit from optimization techniques.

### 7.1. Vectorization

The most crucial optimization is to leverage vectorized operations provided by Pandas and NumPy, which are implemented in C and are significantly faster than Python loops.

*   **Avoid explicit loops:** Instead of iterating row by row, use built-in Pandas/NumPy functions.

    \`\`\`python
    df_perf = pd.DataFrame({'A': np.random.rand(1_000_000), 'B': np.random.rand(1_000_000)})

    # Bad (slow)
    # result = []
    # for i in range(len(df_perf)):
    #     result.append(df_perf['A'].iloc[i] + df_perf['B'].iloc[i])

    # Good (fast - vectorized)
    df_perf['C'] = df_perf['A'] + df_perf['B']
    \`\`\`

*   **Use \`apply()\` sparingly for complex logic:** While \`apply()\` is flexible, it can be slower than vectorized operations. If a vectorized alternative exists, use it.

    \`\`\`python
    # Vectorized string operation (faster)
    df_perf['A_str'] = df_perf['A'].astype(str)
    df_perf['A_upper'] = df_perf['A_str'].str.upper()

    # apply() for string operation (slower)
    # df_perf['A_upper_apply'] = df_perf['A_str'].apply(lambda x: x.upper())
    \`\`\`

### 7.2. Data Types

*   **Use appropriate dtypes:** Smaller data types use less memory and can be faster.
    *   \`int8\`, \`int16\`, \`int32\`, \`int64\`
    *   \`float32\`, \`float64\`
    *   \`category\` for categorical data with a limited number of unique values.
    *   \`bool\` for boolean flags.

    \`\`\`python
    df_types_opt = pd.DataFrame({
        'Small_Int': [1, 2, 3, 4, 5],
        'Large_Float': [1.123456789, 2.0, 3.0, 4.0, 5.0],
        'Category_Col': ['A', 'B', 'A', 'C', 'B']
    })
    print("Original memory usage:\n", df_types_opt.memory_usage(deep=True))

    df_types_opt['Small_Int'] = df_types_opt['Small_Int'].astype('int8')
    df_types_opt['Large_Float'] = df_types_opt['Large_Float'].astype('float32')
    df_types_opt['Category_Col'] = df_types_opt['Category_Col'].astype('category')
    print("\nOptimized memory usage:\n", df_types_opt.memory_usage(deep=True))
    \`\`\`

### 7.3. Chaining Operations

Chaining multiple Pandas operations can sometimes be more efficient as it reduces intermediate DataFrame creation.

\`\`\`python
# Less efficient (creates intermediate DFs)
# df_temp = df[df['Age'] > 25]
# df_final = df_temp.sort_values(by='Name')

# More efficient (chained)
df_chained = df[df['Age'] > 25].sort_values(by='Name').reset_index(drop=True)
print("\nChained operations example:\n", df_chained)
\`\`\`

### 7.4. Using \`.at\` and \`.iat\` for Scalar Access

For accessing single scalar values, \`.at\` (label-based) and \`.iat\` (integer-position-based) are significantly faster than \`loc\` or \`iloc\`.

\`\`\`python
# Accessing a single value with .loc (slower for scalar)
# value_loc = df.loc[0, 'Name']

# Accessing a single value with .at (faster for scalar)
value_at = df.at[0, 'Name']
print(f"\nValue from .at: {value_at}")

# Accessing a single value with .iloc (slower for scalar)
# value_iloc = df.iloc[0, 0]

# Accessing a single value with .iat (faster for scalar)
value_iat = df.iat[0, 0]
print(f"Value from .iat: {value_iat}")
\`\`\`

### 7.5. Reading Large Files in Chunks

For very large CSV/Excel files that don't fit into memory, use the \`chunksize\` parameter in \`pd.read_csv()\` (or similar functions) to process them in smaller batches.

\`\`\`python
# Example (not executable without a large CSV)
# for chunk in pd.read_csv('large_data.csv', chunksize=100000):
#     # Process each chunk
#     processed_chunk = chunk[chunk['column'] > 0]
#     # ... further processing or append to a list
\`\`\`

By understanding and applying these concepts, you can effectively manipulate, clean, and analyze complex datasets using Pandas, laying a solid foundation for more advanced machine learning tasks.

- [video] [Complete Python Pandas Data Science Tutorial! (2025 Updated Edition)](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=2uvysYbKdjM)
- [article] [Pandas Tutorial for Data Analysis - GeeksforGeeks | Videos](https://www.geeksforgeeks.org/videos/pandas-tutorial-for-data-analysis/)

:::


# Data Visualization with Matplotlib & Seaborn

Data visualization is a critical component of the data science workflow, especially during Exploratory Data Analysis (EDA). It allows us to uncover patterns, detect anomalies, test hypotheses, and communicate insights effectively. While numerical summaries and statistical tests provide quantitative understanding, visualizations offer an intuitive and often more powerful way to grasp complex data relationships. Python's ecosystem, particularly with libraries like Matplotlib and Seaborn, provides an unparalleled toolkit for creating a wide range of static, interactive, and animated plots.

This section will delve deep into the capabilities of Matplotlib and Seaborn, starting with the foundational concepts of Matplotlib and then building up to the high-level statistical plotting offered by Seaborn.

## The Importance of Data Visualization in EDA

Exploratory Data Analysis (EDA) is the process of analyzing data sets to summarize their main characteristics, often with visual methods. It's a crucial first step in any data science project, preceding formal modeling or hypothesis testing.

:::tip Key Benefits of Visualization in EDA
*   **Pattern Recognition:** Humans are inherently good at recognizing visual patterns. Visualizations quickly reveal trends, clusters, outliers, and distributions that might be hidden in raw data or tables.
*   **Anomaly Detection:** Outliers, missing values, or data entry errors often stand out clearly in plots, allowing for early identification and correction.
*   **Hypothesis Generation:** Visualizations can inspire new questions and hypotheses about the data, guiding further analysis.
*   **Model Selection:** Understanding data distributions and relationships can inform the choice of appropriate machine learning models. For instance, linear relationships might suggest linear regression, while complex non-linear patterns might point towards tree-based models.
*   **Communication:** Visualizations are an effective way to communicate findings to both technical and non-technical audiences, making complex data accessible and understandable.
:::

## Matplotlib: The Foundation of Python Plotting

Matplotlib is the grandfather of Python plotting libraries. It provides a highly flexible and comprehensive framework for creating static, animated, and interactive visualizations in Python. While often considered lower-level compared to Seaborn, its granular control over every aspect of a plot makes it indispensable for customization and complex plotting scenarios.

### Figure and Axes Objects: The Canvas and the Plot

Understanding Matplotlib's object-oriented interface is key to mastering it. The two fundamental objects are \`Figure\` and \`Axes\`.

*   **\`Figure\`**: This is the top-level container for all plot elements. Think of it as the entire window or page that holds one or more plots. You can control its size, background color, and other overall properties.
*   **\`Axes\`**: This is the actual plotting area where the data is drawn. A \`Figure\` can contain multiple \`Axes\` objects, each representing an individual plot (e.g., in subplots). Each \`Axes\` has its own x-axis, y-axis, title, labels, and plot elements.

Let's illustrate this with a simple example:

\`\`\`python
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
import numpy as np

# Create a Figure and a single Axes object
fig, ax = plt.subplots(figsize=(8, 6)) # figsize sets the width and height of the figure in inches

# Plot data on the Axes
x = np.linspace(0, 10, 100)
y = np.sin(x)
ax.plot(x, y, label='sin(x)', color='blue', linestyle='--')

# Customize the Axes
ax.set_title('Simple Sine Wave')
ax.set_xlabel('X-axis')
ax.set_ylabel('Y-axis')
ax.legend()
ax.grid(True)

# Display the plot
plt.show()
\`\`\`

In this code:
- \`plt.subplots()\` creates both the \`Figure\` (\`fig\`) and an \`Axes\` (\`ax\`) object.
- We then interact directly with the \`ax\` object to plot data (\`ax.plot()\`) and set labels, title, and legend.
- \`plt.show()\` displays the figure.

### Common Plot Types

Matplotlib supports a vast array of plot types. Here are some of the most frequently used for EDA:

#### 1. Scatter Plots (\`plt.scatter()\` or \`ax.scatter()\`)

Scatter plots are used to visualize the relationship between two numerical variables. Each point represents an observation, with its position determined by the values of the two variables. They are excellent for identifying correlations, clusters, and outliers.

\`\`\`python
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
import numpy as np

np.random.seed(42)
x = np.random.rand(50) * 10
y = 2 * x + 1 + np.random.randn(50) * 3 # Linear relationship with noise

fig, ax = plt.subplots(figsize=(8, 6))
ax.scatter(x, y, color='purple', alpha=0.7, edgecolors='w', s=50) # s for size
ax.set_title('Scatter Plot of X vs Y')
ax.set_xlabel('X Value')
ax.set_ylabel('Y Value')
ax.grid(True, linestyle=':', alpha=0.6)
plt.show()
\`\`\`

#### 2. Line Plots (\`plt.plot()\` or \`ax.plot()\`)

Line plots are ideal for showing trends over an ordered dimension, most commonly time series data. They connect data points with lines, making it easy to see changes and patterns.

\`\`\`python
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
import numpy as np
import pandas as pd

# Time series data
dates = pd.date_range(start='2023-01-01', periods=100, freq='D')
values = np.cumsum(np.random.randn(100)) + 50

fig, ax = plt.subplots(figsize=(10, 6))
ax.plot(dates, values, color='green', linestyle='-', linewidth=2, marker='o', markersize=4, label='Daily Stock Price')
ax.set_title('Stock Price Over Time')
ax.set_xlabel('Date')
ax.set_ylabel('Price')
ax.legend()
ax.grid(True, linestyle='--', alpha=0.7)
plt.xticks(rotation=45) # Rotate date labels for readability
plt.tight_layout() # Adjust layout to prevent labels overlapping
plt.show()
\`\`\`

#### 3. Bar Plots (\`plt.bar()\` or \`ax.bar()\`)

Bar plots are used to display and compare the quantities of different categories. The length of each bar is proportional to the value it represents.

\`\`\`python
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt

categories = ['A', 'B', 'C', 'D', 'E']
values = [23, 45, 56, 12, 39]

fig, ax = plt.subplots(figsize=(8, 6))
ax.bar(categories, values, color=['skyblue', 'lightcoral', 'lightgreen', 'gold', 'plum'])
ax.set_title('Bar Plot of Category Values')
ax.set_xlabel('Category')
ax.set_ylabel('Value')
plt.show()
\`\`\`

#### 4. Histograms (\`plt.hist()\` or \`ax.hist()\`)

Histograms are used to visualize the distribution of a single numerical variable. They divide the data into bins and show the frequency or count of data points falling into each bin.

\`\`\`python
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
import numpy as np

data = np.random.normal(loc=0, scale=1, size=1000) # Normally distributed data

fig, ax = plt.subplots(figsize=(8, 6))
ax.hist(data, bins=30, color='teal', edgecolor='black', alpha=0.7)
ax.set_title('Histogram of Random Data')
ax.set_xlabel('Value')
ax.set_ylabel('Frequency')
plt.show()
\`\`\`

#### 5. Box Plots (\`plt.boxplot()\` or \`ax.boxplot()\`)

Box plots (or box-and-whisker plots) display the distribution of a numerical variable and highlight its quartiles, median, and potential outliers. They are excellent for comparing distributions across different categories.

\`\`\`python
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
import numpy as np

# Generate some data for different groups
data_group1 = np.random.normal(loc=0, scale=1, size=100)
data_group2 = np.random.normal(loc=2, scale=1.5, size=100)
data_group3 = np.random.normal(loc=1, scale=0.8, size=100)

data_to_plot = [data_group1, data_group2, data_group3]
labels = ['Group 1', 'Group 2', 'Group 3']

fig, ax = plt.subplots(figsize=(8, 6))
bp = ax.boxplot(data_to_plot, patch_artist=True, labels=labels, vert=True) # vert=True for vertical boxes

# Customize box colors
colors = ['lightblue', 'lightgreen', 'pink']
for patch, color in zip(bp['boxes'], colors):
    patch.set_facecolor(color)

ax.set_title('Box Plot of Data Distributions by Group')
ax.set_xlabel('Group')
ax.set_ylabel('Value')
ax.grid(axis='y', linestyle='--', alpha=0.7)
plt.show()
\`\`\`

#### 6. Violin Plots (\`ax.violinplot()\`)

Violin plots are similar to box plots but provide a richer view of the distribution by showing the probability density of the data at different values. They combine the features of a box plot with a kernel density estimate.

\`\`\`python
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
import numpy as np

data_group1 = np.random.normal(loc=0, scale=1, size=100)
data_group2 = np.random.normal(loc=2, scale=1.5, size=100)
data_group3 = np.random.normal(loc=1, scale=0.8, size=100)

data_to_plot = [data_group1, data_group2, data_group3]
labels = ['Group 1', 'Group 2', 'Group 3']

fig, ax = plt.subplots(figsize=(8, 6))
vp = ax.violinplot(data_to_plot, showmeans=True, showmedians=True, showextrema=True)

# Customize violin colors (optional, more complex than boxplot)
for idx, body in enumerate(vp['bodies']):
    body.set_facecolor(colors[idx])
    body.set_edgecolor('black')
    body.set_alpha(0.7)

ax.set_xticks(np.arange(1, len(labels) + 1)) # Set x-ticks manually for labels
ax.set_xticklabels(labels)
ax.set_title('Violin Plot of Data Distributions by Group')
ax.set_xlabel('Group')
ax.set_ylabel('Value')
ax.grid(axis='y', linestyle='--', alpha=0.7)
plt.show()
\`\`\`

### Customization: Making Plots Informative and Aesthetic

Matplotlib offers extensive customization options to make your plots clear, professional, and visually appealing.

#### 1. Titles and Labels

*   \`ax.set_title('My Plot Title')\`: Sets the title of the Axes.
*   \`ax.set_xlabel('X-axis Label')\`: Sets the label for the x-axis.
*   \`ax.set_ylabel('Y-axis Label')\`: Sets the label for the y-axis.

You can also use \`plt.title()\`, \`plt.xlabel()\`, \`plt.ylabel()\` when working with the procedural \`pyplot\` interface, but the object-oriented approach (\`ax.set_...\`) is generally preferred for more complex plots and subplots.

#### 2. Legends

Legends are essential when plotting multiple data series on the same Axes.

*   \`ax.plot(x, y1, label='Series 1')\`
*   \`ax.plot(x, y2, label='Series 2')\`
*   \`ax.legend()\`: Displays the legend based on the \`label\` arguments given to plot functions. You can control its location (e.g., \`loc='upper right'\`).

#### 3. Colors and Styles

*   **Colors**: Can be specified by name (e.g., \`'red'\`, \`'blue'\`), hex codes (e.g., \`'#FF0000'\`), or RGB tuples.
*   **Linestyle**: \`linestyle='-'\` (solid), \`'--'\` (dashed), \`'-.'\` (dash-dot), \`':'\` (dotted).
*   **Marker**: \`marker='o'\` (circle), \`'x'\`, \`'s'\` (square), \`'^'\` (triangle up).
*   **Linewidth, Markersize, Alpha**: \`linewidth=2\`, \`markersize=5\`, \`alpha=0.7\` (transparency).

Example:

\`\`\`python
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
import numpy as np

x = np.linspace(0, 2 * np.pi, 100)
y1 = np.sin(x)
y2 = np.cos(x)

fig, ax = plt.subplots(figsize=(9, 5))
ax.plot(x, y1, color='darkblue', linestyle='--', linewidth=2, marker='o', markersize=5, label='Sine')
ax.plot(x, y2, color='red', linestyle='-', linewidth=1.5, marker='x', markersize=6, label='Cosine', alpha=0.8)

ax.set_title('Sine and Cosine Waves', fontsize=16, fontweight='bold')
ax.set_xlabel('Angle (radians)', fontsize=12)
ax.set_ylabel('Amplitude', fontsize=12)
ax.legend(loc='upper right', shadow=True, fancybox=True)
ax.grid(True, linestyle=':', alpha=0.6)
ax.set_xlim(0, 2 * np.pi)
ax.set_ylim(-1.2, 1.2)
plt.show()
\`\`\`

#### 4. Text and Annotations

You can add arbitrary text or annotations to specific points on your plot using \`ax.text()\` and \`ax.annotate()\`.

\`\`\`python
fig, ax = plt.subplots(figsize=(7, 5))
ax.plot(x, y1, 'b-')
ax.text(np.pi/2, 1.05, 'Max Value', horizontalalignment='center', color='red', fontsize=12)
ax.annotate('Min Value', xy=(3*np.pi/2, -1), xytext=(3*np.pi/2 + 0.5, -0.8),
            arrowprops=dict(facecolor='black', shrink=0.05),
            fontsize=12, color='green')
plt.show()
\`\`\`

### Subplots: Multiple Plots in One Figure

Subplots are essential for comparing different aspects of your data side-by-side. Matplotlib provides \`plt.subplots()\` for creating grids of Axes objects.

The syntax \`fig, axes = plt.subplots(nrows, ncols)\` creates a figure and a grid of \`nrows * ncols\` axes. \`axes\` will be a NumPy array of Axes objects.

\`\`\`python
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
import numpy as np

x = np.linspace(0, 10, 100)
y1 = np.sin(x)
y2 = np.cos(x)
y3 = x
y4 = x**2

fig, axes = plt.subplots(nrows=2, ncols=2, figsize=(12, 10))

# Plot on the first Axes (top-left)
axes[0, 0].plot(x, y1, color='blue')
axes[0, 0].set_title('Sine Wave')
axes[0, 0].set_xlabel('X')
axes[0, 0].set_ylabel('sin(X)')

# Plot on the second Axes (top-right)
axes[0, 1].plot(x, y2, color='red')
axes[0, 1].set_title('Cosine Wave')
axes[0, 1].set_xlabel('X')
axes[0, 1].set_ylabel('cos(X)')

# Plot on the third Axes (bottom-left)
axes[1, 0].plot(x, y3, color='green')
axes[1, 0].set_title('Linear Function')
axes[1, 0].set_xlabel('X')
axes[1, 0].set_ylabel('X')

# Plot on the fourth Axes (bottom-right)
axes[1, 1].plot(x, y4, color='purple')
axes[1, 1].set_title('Quadratic Function')
axes[1, 1].set_xlabel('X')
axes[1, 1].set_ylabel('X^2')

plt.tight_layout() # Adjust subplot params for a tight layout
plt.show()
\`\`\`

You can also use \`plt.subplot(nrows, ncols, index)\` for a more procedural approach, but \`plt.subplots()\` is generally preferred for its object-oriented return value.

### Animation Basics

Matplotlib can also create basic animations, which can be useful for visualizing changes over time or iterating through parameters. This typically involves \`matplotlib.animation.FuncAnimation\`.

\`\`\`python
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
import numpy as np
from matplotlib.animation import FuncAnimation

# Create figure and axes
fig, ax = plt.subplots(figsize=(8, 6))
x_data, y_data = [], []
line, = ax.plot([], [], 'r-')

def init():
    ax.set_xlim(0, 2 * np.pi)
    ax.set_ylim(-1.1, 1.1)
    return line,

def update(frame):
    x_data.append(frame)
    y_data.append(np.sin(frame))
    line.set_data(x_data, y_data)
    return line,

# Create animation
ani = FuncAnimation(fig, update, frames=np.linspace(0, 2 * np.pi, 128),
                    init_func=init, blit=True, interval=50)

# To save the animation (requires imagemagick or ffmpeg)
# ani.save('sine_wave.gif', writer='imagemagick', fps=30)

plt.show()
\`\`\`
:::note Animation Dependencies
Saving animations often requires external dependencies like \`ffmpeg\` (for MP4) or \`ImageMagick\` (for GIF). These need to be installed on your system and accessible in your PATH.
:::

## Seaborn: High-Level Statistical Plotting

Seaborn is a high-level data visualization library based on Matplotlib. It provides a more convenient and aesthetically pleasing interface for drawing attractive and informative statistical graphics. Seaborn is particularly adept at working with Pandas DataFrames, making it a natural choice for EDA. It abstracts away much of the Matplotlib boilerplate, allowing you to create complex plots with just a few lines of code.

### Integration with Pandas DataFrames

One of Seaborn's greatest strengths is its tight integration with Pandas DataFrames. You can often pass entire DataFrames or specific column names directly to Seaborn functions, and it will handle the mapping of data to visual elements.

\`\`\`python
import seaborn as sns
import pandas as pd
import numpy as np
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt

# Create a sample DataFrame
data = {
    'Category': np.random.choice(['A', 'B', 'C'], size=100),
    'Value': np.random.randn(100) * 10 + 50,
    'Group': np.random.choice(['X', 'Y'], size=100),
    'Time': pd.to_datetime(pd.date_range(start='2023-01-01', periods=100, freq='D'))
}
df = pd.DataFrame(data)

# Example: Using Seaborn with a DataFrame
plt.figure(figsize=(8, 6))
sns.boxplot(x='Category', y='Value', data=df)
plt.title('Distribution of Value by Category')
plt.show()
\`\`\`

### High-Level Statistical Plotting Functions

Seaborn organizes its functions into several categories, making it easy to choose the right plot for your data type and analytical goal.

#### 1. Relational Plots (\`sns.relplot()\`)

\`relplot()\` is a figure-level function for visualizing statistical relationships between variables. It can create scatter plots and line plots, and importantly, it can automatically handle faceting (creating multiple subplots based on categorical variables).

*   **Scatter Plot (default)**: Shows the relationship between two numerical variables.
*   **Line Plot (\`kind='line'\`)**: Shows the evolution of one variable with respect to another, often time.

\`\`\`python
# Scatter plot with hue and size
plt.figure(figsize=(10, 7)) # Note: relplot creates its own figure, so plt.figure here is just for context
sns.relplot(x='Value', y='Time', hue='Category', size='Value', sizes=(20, 400), data=df, kind='scatter', aspect=1.5)
plt.title('Relational Scatter Plot of Value over Time by Category', y=1.05) # y adjusts title position for figure-level plots
plt.show()

# Line plot with faceting
tips = sns.load_dataset('tips') # Load a built-in dataset
sns.relplot(x='total_bill', y='tip', col='time', hue='smoker', style='smoker', size='size', data=tips, kind='scatter', aspect=0.8)
plt.suptitle('Tips by Total Bill, Time of Day, and Smoker Status', y=1.02)
plt.show()
\`\`\`

#### 2. Distribution Plots (\`sns.displot()\`)

\`displot()\` is a figure-level function for visualizing the distribution of a single variable. It can draw histograms, kernel density estimates (KDEs), and empirical cumulative distribution functions (ECDFs).

*   **Histogram (default)**: \`kind='hist'\`
*   **KDE Plot**: \`kind='kde'\`
*   **ECDF Plot**: \`kind='ecdf'\`

\`\`\`python
# Histogram with faceting by Group
sns.displot(x='Value', col='Group', data=df, kde=True, bins=15, height=5, aspect=1.2)
plt.suptitle('Distribution of Value by Group', y=1.02)
plt.show()

# KDE plot
plt.figure(figsize=(8, 6))
sns.kdeplot(x='Value', hue='Category', data=df, fill=True, common_norm=False)
plt.title('KDE Plot of Value by Category')
plt.show()
\`\`\`

#### 3. Categorical Plots (\`sns.catplot()\`)

\`catplot()\` is a figure-level function for visualizing relationships between a numerical variable and one or more categorical variables. It offers various plot types:

*   **Stripplot**: \`kind='strip'\` (scatter plot with jitter to avoid overlap)
*   **Swarmplot**: \`kind='swarm'\` (similar to stripplot but points are adjusted to not overlap)
*   **Boxplot**: \`kind='box'\`
*   **Violinplot**: \`kind='violin'\`
*   **Boxenplot**: \`kind='boxen'\` (enhanced box plot for larger datasets)
*   **Pointplot**: \`kind='point'\` (shows point estimates and confidence intervals)
*   **Barplot**: \`kind='bar'\` (shows mean/median and confidence intervals)

\`\`\`python
# Swarmplot with hue
sns.catplot(x='Category', y='Value', hue='Group', data=df, kind='swarm', height=6, aspect=1.2)
plt.suptitle('Swarm Plot of Value by Category and Group', y=1.02)
plt.show()

# Violin plot with faceting
sns.catplot(x='Group', y='Value', col='Category', data=df, kind='violin', inner='quartile', height=5, aspect=0.8)
plt.suptitle('Violin Plot of Value by Group across Categories', y=1.02)
plt.show()
\`\`\`

#### 4. Joint Plots (\`sns.jointplot()\`)

\`jointplot()\` combines the display of two variables with their marginal distributions. It's excellent for exploring bivariate relationships and their individual distributions simultaneously.

*   **Scatter (default)**: \`kind='scatter'\`
*   **KDE**: \`kind='kde'\`
*   **Hexbin**: \`kind='hex'\` (for dense data)
*   **Regression**: \`kind='reg'\`

\`\`\`python
iris = sns.load_dataset('iris') # Load a built-in dataset

# Scatter plot with marginal histograms
sns.jointplot(x='sepal_length', y='sepal_width', data=iris, hue='species', kind='scatter', height=7)
plt.suptitle('Joint Plot of Sepal Length and Width by Species', y=1.02)
plt.show()

# KDE plot with marginal KDEs
sns.jointplot(x='petal_length', y='petal_width', data=iris, kind='kde', fill=True, cmap='viridis', height=7)
plt.suptitle('Joint KDE Plot of Petal Length and Width', y=1.02)
plt.show()
\`\`\`

#### 5. Pair Plots (\`sns.pairplot()\`)

\`pairplot()\` creates a grid of scatter plots for all pairwise relationships between numerical variables in a DataFrame, and histograms/KDEs for the univariate distributions on the diagonal. It's a powerful tool for quickly surveying relationships in a dataset.

\`\`\`python
# Pair plot for the Iris dataset, colored by species
sns.pairplot(iris, hue='species', diag_kind='kde')
plt.suptitle('Pair Plot of Iris Dataset by Species', y=1.02)
plt.show()
\`\`\`

#### 6. Heatmaps (\`sns.heatmap()\`)

Heatmaps are used to visualize matrix-like data, where the intensity of color represents the value in each cell. They are particularly useful for correlation matrices.

\`\`\`python
# Calculate correlation matrix
correlation_matrix = iris.drop('species', axis=1).corr()

plt.figure(figsize=(8, 7))
sns.heatmap(correlation_matrix, annot=True, cmap='coolwarm', fmt=".2f", linewidths=.5)
plt.title('Correlation Matrix of Iris Features')
plt.show()
\`\`\`

### Theme Management and Aesthetics

Seaborn significantly improves the aesthetics of Matplotlib plots. It comes with several built-in themes and functions to control the overall look of your plots.

*   **\`sns.set_theme()\`**: Configures Matplotlib's rcParams to apply Seaborn's aesthetic. You can specify \`style\` (e.g., \`'darkgrid'\`, \`'whitegrid'\`, \`'dark'\`, \`'white'\`, \`'ticks'\`) and \`palette\`.
*   **Context**: \`sns.set_context()\` allows scaling plot elements for different presentation contexts (e.g., \`'paper'\`, \`'notebook'\`, \`'talk'\`, \`'poster'\`).
*   **Palettes**: \`sns.color_palette()\` and \`sns.set_palette()\` manage the color schemes used in plots. Seaborn offers a wide range of perceptually uniform colormaps and qualitative palettes.

\`\`\`python
# Set a different theme and palette
sns.set_theme(style='whitegrid', palette='pastel')

plt.figure(figsize=(8, 6))
sns.histplot(x='Value', hue='Category', data=df, kde=True, bins=15)
plt.title('Histogram with Seaborn Theme')
plt.show()

# Reset to default Matplotlib settings (if needed)
# plt.rcdefaults()
# sns.reset_orig()
\`\`\`

### Integration with Matplotlib for Further Customization

While Seaborn provides high-level functions, it builds directly on Matplotlib. This means you can use Matplotlib functions to further customize Seaborn plots. Seaborn functions often return Matplotlib \`Axes\` objects (for \`scatterplot\`, \`histplot\`, \`boxplot\`, etc.) or \`Figure\` objects (for \`relplot\`, \`displot\`, \`catplot\`, \`jointplot\`, \`pairplot\`).

\`\`\`python
# Create a Seaborn plot
plt.figure(figsize=(10, 6))
ax = sns.scatterplot(x='total_bill', y='tip', hue='time', style='smoker', data=tips, s=100)

# Further customize using Matplotlib Axes methods
ax.set_title('Tips vs. Total Bill by Time and Smoker Status', fontsize=16, color='darkred')
ax.set_xlabel('Total Bill ($)', fontsize=12)
ax.set_ylabel('Tip ($)', fontsize=12)
ax.tick_params(axis='x', rotation=45) # Rotate x-axis ticks
ax.grid(axis='y', linestyle='--', alpha=0.7)

# Add an annotation
ax.annotate('Higher tips on dinner?', xy=(25, 5), xytext=(35, 8),
            arrowprops=dict(facecolor='black', shrink=0.05),
            fontsize=10, color='blue')

plt.tight_layout()
plt.show()
\`\`\`

## Conclusion

Matplotlib and Seaborn are powerful and complementary libraries for data visualization in Python. Matplotlib provides the foundational, granular control necessary for any plot, while Seaborn builds upon it to offer high-level, aesthetically pleasing, and statistically oriented plots, particularly useful for exploring relationships within Pandas DataFrames. Mastering both will equip you with a versatile toolkit to effectively visualize and communicate insights from your data during EDA and beyond. The choice between them often depends on the level of control required and the complexity of the statistical relationships being explored. For quick, beautiful statistical plots, Seaborn is often the go-to; for highly customized or complex multi-panel figures, Matplotlib's object-oriented interface provides the ultimate flexibility.
